<!--
Hi everyone! I’m Josh – I’m a full time independent open source maintainer. I focus on static analysis tooling around JavaScript & TypeScript – most notably, typescript-eslint, the tooling that allows you to run ESLint and Prettier on TypeScript code. 
I’m also the author of the recent Learning TypeScript book, published by O’Reilly.
-->

<!--
We’re going to attack this problem in three steps, increasing in scale as we go along.
We’ll start with the philosophical side: understanding for ourselves what it means to build a design system. We’ll cover the layers that go into a design system, organized nicely in a structure known as Atomic Design. 
We’ll then cover the technical side of things – running through a simulated rollout of a design system in an existing webpage, and then showing tips for how to create design-system-friendly components with TypeScript.
Lastly, we’ll cover organizational best practices: making sure your design system is communicated to folks around you in ways they can understand and gain experience on.
-->

-->

<!--
Let’s take a look at the Codecademy learner dashboard page, which showcases a few design system practices. This slide’s background image is a screenshot of codecademy.com/learn for a signed-in user.
For many, “design system” means the low-level “atoms” that form the basic building blocks of the page: colors, buttons, typography, and iconography. That’s a huge component of many design systems and is incredibly important to get right. This page has quite a few examples:
Colors: white, gray, etc.
Text presets: caption, menu, h2, h3, body
Buttons: “TextButton” and “FillButton”
Iconography and logos
More unusual or niche ones: a ProgressBar and a HexCharacter
-->

transition: { name: view-transition, duration: 500 }
---

<!--
Let me give you an example of the kind of issues you might find without a type system. Suppose you’re declaring a button component: here implemented as a function component in your favorite JSX-based framework, such as React or Solid, and some styling solution that uses a ‘css’ prop. All this component is meant to allow is customizing the background based on a ‘color’ prop.
Nothing in your developers’ editors will guide them into using this component properly. They might provide a classname, or give a color string that doesn’t exist. That means they’ll have to look at the source code and/or try out the component in isolation to learn how it works – which can take up a lot of time when there are a lot of components and they’re more complex than this trivial example!
-->

<!--
TypeScript type annotations get pretty powerful. Here, this button declares that it allows any props from the normal React HTML Attributes for an HTML button element… plus a variant prop that may be “primary”, “secondary”, or “outline”. TypeScript can yell at you if you provide a prop that’s not either of those, or provide a prop that doesn’t match one of those types.
-->

---
layout: none
transition: { name: fade, duration: 700 }
---

-->
